**Lemma 26** (Faces with at most two active incidences). *Let $h=\max_{e\subset j}h_e$. If $j$ has at most two active incidences, then <a id="eq:tail-lower-active"></a>$$\label{eq:tail-lower-active}
 S_j\ge0,
 \qquad S_j\ge\frac{h^2}{24}-\frac h{12}.$$ Equality $S_j=0$ requires $c_j=0$ and either all three pair dimensions zero or exactly one pair dimension equal to $2$. The latter case requires exactly two active incidences, at that pair’s endpoints.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tail-lower-active"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-lower-active</summary>

<a id="eq:tail-baseline-lower"></a>
*Proof.* Put $m=\max_\ell s_\ell$ and choose $\ell_0$ with $s_{\ell_0}=m$. Using $h=c+m$ and $\sum s_\ell\le D-c$, expand $$\begin{aligned}
 3C-\sum_\ell b(c+s_\ell)-2b(c+m)
 &=3(D+1)c-5b(c)
   -c\left(\sum_\ell s_\ell+2m\right)
   +2\sum_{\ell\ne\ell_0}b(s_\ell)\\
 &\ge b(c)+2\sum_{\ell\ne\ell_0}b(s_\ell).
\end{aligned}$$ The last step uses $\sum s_\ell+2m\le3(D-c)$; substituting this upper bound leaves exactly $b(c)$ in the terms involving $c$. Consequently $$\label{eq:tail-baseline-lower}
 \beta_j\ge\frac{b(h)}3+\frac{b(c)}6
             +\frac13\sum_{\ell\ne\ell_0}b(s_\ell).$$ The gain is zero with zero or one active incidence. With two, it is $b(\lfloor h'/2\rfloor)$ for their pair dimension $h'\le h$. For an integer $h\ge0$, $$b(\lfloor h/2\rfloor)\le\frac{b(h)}3,
 \qquad b(\lfloor h/2\rfloor)\le\frac{h(h+2)}8.$$ The first follows separately for $h=2k$ and $h=2k+1$; its differences are $k(k-1)/6$ and $(k+1)(k+2)/6$, respectively, so equality holds exactly for $h=0,2$. Combining these inequalities with Equation <a href="#eq:tail-baseline-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-baseline-lower">[eq:tail-baseline-lower]</a> proves nonnegativity and $S_j\ge b(h)/3-h(h+2)/8=h^2/24-h/12$. Equality forces $c=0$, $s_\ell=0$ for $\ell\ne\ell_0$, and $h=0$ or $2$. When $h=2$, the gain must equal $1$, which requires the stated two active endpoints. With at most one active incidence the positive baseline for $h>0$ excludes equality. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:tail-feasible"></a>
For a face with three active incidences, retain the notation of Section <a href="#sec:planted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:planted">6</a>: $$\begin{gathered}
 u,\quad r'=r-u,\quad\delta=2u-c=d-2r',\quad
 s_\ell=p_\ell+t_\ell,\\
 P=\sum_\ell p_\ell,\quad T=\sum_\ell t_\ell,
 \quad w=\frac{d-3\delta}{2}-P+v.
 \end{gathered}$$ The necessary constraints, which will suffice below, are $$\label{eq:tail-feasible}
 \begin{gathered}
 c\ge|\delta|,\qquad P+T\le d,\qquad
 p_\ell,t_\ell,v\ge0,\qquad
 t_\ell,v\le p_\ell+\delta,\qquad w\ge0.
 \end{gathered}$$ In particular $T\le P+3\delta$, and hence $d+3\delta\ge P+T+3\delta\ge2T\ge0$. All the dimensions are integers, and $\delta=2u-c$ with $0\le u\le c$.

<a id="eq:slack"></a>
Substituting the gain in Equation <a href="#eq:planted-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planted-gain">[eq:planted-gain]</a> into $S=\beta-g$ gives the exact identity $$\label{eq:slack}
 \begin{split}
 S={}&\frac{c(d+\delta-1)-\delta(d+2)+2\delta^2}{4}\\
 &+\sum_{\ell=1}^3\left[
 \frac{(2p_\ell-t_\ell)(2p_\ell-t_\ell-1)}6
 +\delta p_\ell-\frac c6(p_\ell+t_\ell)\right]
 +vw-b(v).
 \end{split}$$ For clarity, the algebra separates into two identities. The common-space part uses $D=d+c$ and $u=(c+\delta)/2$: $$\frac{(D+1)c}{2}-\frac{b(c)}2
 -\big((r+c+1)u-2u^2\big)
 =\frac{c(d+\delta-1)-\delta(d+2)+2\delta^2}{4}.$$ For each reduced pair, use $b(c+s)=b(c)+b(s)+cs$ and $$p^2+b(t)-\frac23b(p+t)
 =\frac{(2p-t)(2p-t-1)}6.$$ These expansions yield every term of Equation <a href="#eq:slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slack">[eq:slack]</a>.

<a id="eq:tail-linear-loss"></a>
Let $Q$ be the homogeneous quadratic part of its right-hand side: <a id="eq:tail-quadratic"></a>$$\label{eq:tail-quadratic}
\begin{split}
 Q={}&\frac{c(d+\delta)-\delta d+2\delta^2}{4}\\
 &+\sum_{\ell=1}^3\left[
 \frac{(2p_\ell-t_\ell)^2}{6}
 +\delta p_\ell-\frac c6(p_\ell+t_\ell)\right]
 +v\left(\frac{d-3\delta}{2}-P\right)+\frac{v^2}{2}.
\end{split}$$ The remaining terms are linear: $$\label{eq:tail-linear-loss}
 S=Q-\frac c4-\frac\delta2-\frac{2P-T}{6}-\frac v2.$$

The next estimate will turn the total slack into bounds on $P,T,\delta$. When a pair dimension grows, these bounds force its common part to spread through neighboring faces until the excess separation is too large. Once all pair dimensions are bounded, we return to the exact slack and use its coefficient of $d$.

<a id="stmt-lem-coercivity"></a>
<div id="lem:coercivity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Coercivity). *Every tuple satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:tail-feasible" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-feasible">[eq:tail-feasible]</a> satisfies <a id="eq:coercivity"></a>$$\label{eq:coercivity}
 Q\ge\frac1{342}(P^2+T^2+\delta^2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-coercivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coercivity</summary>

<a id="eq:tail-scalar-quadratic"></a>
*Proof.* Set $$a=\left(P-\frac{d-3\delta}{2}\right)_+,
 \qquad v_0=\min\left(\frac P3+\delta,
                         \frac{P-T+3\delta}{2}\right).$$ Both arguments of the minimum are nonnegative, by Equation <a href="#eq:tail-feasible" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-feasible">[eq:tail-feasible]</a>. Completing the square gives $$vw-\frac{v^2}{2}
 =\left(\frac{d-3\delta}{2}-P\right)v+\frac{v^2}{2}
 \ge-\frac{a^2}{2}.$$ The condition $w\ge0$ gives $a\le v$; furthermore $v\le\min_\ell(p_\ell+\delta)\le P/3+\delta$. From $d\ge P+T$ we also obtain $a\le(P-T+3\delta)/2$. Therefore $a\le v_0$, and $$\label{eq:tail-scalar-quadratic}
 \begin{split}
 Q\ge{}&\frac{c(d+\delta)-\delta d+2\delta^2}{4}
 +\frac{(2P-T)^2}{18}+\delta P
 -\frac{c(P+T)}6-\frac{v_0^2}{2}.
 \end{split}$$ Here we also used $\sum_\ell(2p_\ell-t_\ell)^2\ge(2P-T)^2/3$. The coefficient of $c$ on the right is $$\frac{d+\delta}{4}-\frac{P+T}{6}
 \ge\frac{d+3\delta}{12}\ge0.$$ Because $c\ge|\delta|$, replacing $c$ by $|\delta|$ can only decrease the right side. We do so and treat the two signs of $\delta$.

First suppose $\delta\ge0$. The remainder in Equation <a href="#eq:tail-scalar-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-scalar-quadratic">[eq:tail-scalar-quadratic]</a> becomes $$F(T)=\frac{3\delta^2}{4}+\frac{(2P-T)^2}{18}
            +\frac{(5P-T)\delta}{6}-\frac12
            \min\left(\frac P3+\delta,
                        \frac{P-T+3\delta}{2}\right)^2,
 \qquad 0\le T\le P+3\delta.$$ On $0\le T\le P/3+\delta$, the first argument attains the minimum, and $F'(T)=(T-2P)/9-\delta/6\le0$; at the right endpoint this derivative is $-5P/27-\delta/18$. On the remaining interval the coefficient of $T^2$ is $1/18-1/8=-5/72$, so $F$ is concave. Its minimum is therefore at one of the following two endpoints: $$\begin{aligned}
 F(P/3+\delta)&=\frac{32P^2+84P\delta+45\delta^2}{324},\\
 F(P+3\delta)&=\frac{2P^2+12P\delta+27\delta^2}{36}.
\end{aligned}$$ Both are at least $(P^2+\delta^2)/18$. Moreover $$P^2+T^2+\delta^2
 \le P^2+(P+3\delta)^2+\delta^2
 \le3P^2+19\delta^2\le19(P^2+\delta^2).$$ This proves the desired coefficient $1/(18\cdot19)$ in this case, including $P=\delta=0$.

Next suppose $\delta=-y<0$. Put $x=P-3y\ge0$; then $0\le T\le x$. After setting $c=y$, the coefficient of $d$ is $y/2$. Using $d\ge P+T=x+3y+T$ in Equation <a href="#eq:tail-scalar-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-scalar-quadratic">[eq:tail-scalar-quadratic]</a> leaves $$G(T)=\frac{y^2}{4}+\frac{(2x-T)y}{3}
 +\frac{(2x-T)^2}{18}
 -\frac12\min\left(\frac x3,\frac{x-T}{2}\right)^2.$$ Its derivative up to $T=x/3$ is $(T-2x)/9-y/3\le0$; after that breakpoint its quadratic coefficient is again $-5/72$. Thus its minimum is at least the smaller of its breakpoint and last-endpoint values, $$\begin{aligned}
 G(x/3)&=\frac{32x^2+180xy+81y^2}{324},\\
 G(x)&=\frac{2x^2+12xy+9y^2}{36}.
\end{aligned}$$ Each is at least $(x^2+y^2)/18$, including the degenerate interval $x=0$. Finally, $$P^2+T^2+y^2\le(x+3y)^2+x^2+y^2
                 \le3x^2+19y^2\le19(x^2+y^2).$$ The same constant $1/342$ follows, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Uniform control and equality profiles

<a id="eq:tail-uniform-linear"></a>
<a id="eq:tail-budget"></a>
Put $M=\max_{e\in E}h_e$. On every face with three active incidences, $c\le M$, $P\le3(M-c)$, $\delta\le c$, and $v\le p_\ell+\delta\le s_\ell+c=h_\ell\le M$. Equation <a href="#eq:tail-linear-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-linear-loss">[eq:tail-linear-loss]</a> consequently gives the uniform bound $$\label{eq:tail-uniform-linear}
 S\ge Q-\frac{3M}{2}.$$ Indeed the subtracted expression is at most $c/4+c/2+(M-c)+M/2=3M/2-c/4$. Suppose that the cost in Equation <a href="#eq:global-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-cost">[eq:global-cost]</a> does not yet give a strict saving, so $\sum_jS_j+\mathcal E\le0$. Let $J_3$ denote the faces with three active incidences and let $h_j=\max_{e\subset j}h_e$. Summing Lemma <a href="#lem:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coercivity">27</a>, Equation <a href="#eq:tail-uniform-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-uniform-linear">[eq:tail-uniform-linear]</a>, and Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-lower-active" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-lower-active">26</a> gives $$\label{eq:tail-budget}
 \begin{split}
 &\sum_{j\in J_3}
 \frac{P_j^2+T_j^2+\delta_j^2}{342}
 +\sum_{j\notin J_3}\frac{h_j^2}{24}+\mathcal E
 \le33M.
 \end{split}$$ To see the constant explicitly, each three-active face can lose at most $3M/2$, and each lower-activity face loses at most $h_j/12\le M/12\le3M/2$; there are twenty-two faces in total. Every term on the left of Equation <a href="#eq:tail-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-budget">[eq:tail-budget]</a> is nonnegative. Thus it bounds each term separately, despite the possible negative individual slacks.

<a id="stmt-lem-tail-residue"></a>
<div id="lem:tail-residue" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:tail-residual-profile"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Profiles not separated by the first cost). *There is an even-dimensional threshold such that, for every even $D$ above it, every feasible profile and every activity set for which $\sum_jg_j\ge\mathcal C$ has the following properties: $$\label{eq:tail-residual-profile}
 c_j=0\quad(j\in J),\qquad h_e\in\{0,2\}\quad(e\in E),
 \qquad\sum_jg_j\le\frac23\sum_{e\in E}b(h_e).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tail-residue"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-residue</summary>

*Proof.* Assume $\sum_j S_j+\mathcal E\le0$. We first bound all pair dimensions independently of $D$, the activity set, and the choices of maximizing tuples.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### A uniform bound for pair dimensions.

Equation <a href="#eq:tail-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-budget">[eq:tail-budget]</a> gives a constant $K\ge1$, depending only on the fixed numerical coefficients, such that $$P_j+T_j\le K\sqrt M\quad(j\in J_3),\qquad
 h_j\le K\sqrt M\quad(j\notin J_3),\qquad
 \mathcal E\le33M.$$ The case $M=0$ already has the required bound. Suppose $M>0$, and choose a face $j_0$ containing a pair of dimension $M$. For sufficiently large $M$, this face cannot have lower activity, because $M>K\sqrt M$. On a three-active face every reduced pair dimension satisfies $s_\ell\le P+T\le K\sqrt M$. Thus a pair of dimension $h$ on that face forces its common dimension, and hence every pair dimension on the face, to be at least $h-K\sqrt M$.

Let $\ell_*$ be the diameter of the fixed connected face-neighbor graph. We claim by induction on $t$ that every face at distance $t$ from $j_0$ is three-active and every pair on it has dimension at least $M-(t+1)K\sqrt M$. The preceding paragraph gives the case $t=0$. When $M$ is large enough that $$M-(\ell_*+1)K\sqrt M\ge M/2>K\sqrt M,$$ choose for a face at distance $t+1$ its predecessor on a shortest path from $j_0$. Their shared pair has dimension greater than $K\sqrt M$, so the new face cannot have lower activity. Applying the three-active bound proves the claimed lower bound at distance $t+1$. Induction therefore gives $h_e\ge M/2$ for every pair $e$.

Fix a pair $e_*$ with $a_{e_*}>1/3$. Its contribution to $\mathcal E$ is at least $$\frac12(a_{e_*}-1/3)b(h_{e_*})
 \ge \frac{a_{e_*}-1/3}{16}M^2.$$ For large $M$ this contradicts $\mathcal E\le33M$. Hence there is an integer $M_*$, independent of $D$, the activity set, and the maximizing tuples, such that every profile under consideration satisfies $M\le M_*$.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### Choose the dimension after this bound.

On a three-active face with $M\le M_*$, the integers $c,p_\ell,t_\ell,v$ lie between $0$ and $M_*$, and $|\delta|\le M_*$. Put $n_\ell=2p_\ell-t_\ell$. Substituting the definition of $w$ into Equation <a href="#eq:slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slack">[eq:slack]</a> gives <a id="eq:tail-leading-coefficient"></a>$$\label{eq:tail-leading-coefficient}
 S=Ad+R,\qquad
 A=\frac{c-\delta+2v}{4}=\frac{c-u+v}{2},$$ where $$\begin{split}
 R={}&\frac{c(\delta-1)-2\delta+2\delta^2}{4}
 +\frac16\sum_{\ell=1}^3
 \left[n_\ell(n_\ell-1)+6\delta p_\ell-c(p_\ell+t_\ell)\right]\\
 &-\left(\frac{3\delta}{2}+P\right)v+\frac{v(v-1)}2.
\end{split}$$ Let $C_*$ be the maximum of $|R|$ over the finite integer box $0\le c,p_\ell,t_\ell,v\le M_*$, $|\delta|\le M_*$, with $P=\sum_\ell p_\ell$. This bounds every actual remainder and is independent of $D$.

For an actual tuple, $0\le u\le c$ and $v\ge0$, so $A\ge0$. Moreover $c,u,v$ are integers: either $A=0$ or $A\ge1/2$. Choose an even $D$ with $$D>M_*+44C_*.$$ If a three-active face had $A>0$, it would have $S\ge(D-M_*)/2-C_*$. Every other three-active face has $S\ge-C_*$, every lower-activity face has nonnegative slack, and $\mathcal E\ge0$. Since there are $22$ faces, the total slack would be at least $$\frac{D-M_*}{2}-22C_*>0,$$ contrary to the hypothesis. Thus every three-active face has $u=c$, $\delta=c$, and $v=0$.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### Integer equality profiles.

For these parameters, Equation <a href="#eq:slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slack">[eq:slack]</a> becomes <a id="eq:tail-integer-slack"></a>$$\label{eq:tail-integer-slack}
 S=\frac{3c^2-3c}{4}
    +\frac16\sum_{\ell=1}^3
       \left[n_\ell^2+(c-1)n_\ell+3cp_\ell\right].$$ If $c\ge2$, completing the square in $n_\ell$ gives $$S\ge\frac{3c(c-1)}4-\frac{(c-1)^2}{8}
       =\frac{(c-1)(5c+1)}8>0.$$ If $c=1$, then $S=\sum_\ell(n_\ell^2+3p_\ell)/6\ge0$, and equality forces $p_\ell=t_\ell=0$ for every $\ell$. If $c=0$, the constraint $t_\ell\le p_\ell$ gives the integer $n_\ell\ge p_\ell\ge0$. Each $n_\ell(n_\ell-1)$ is nonnegative, and its zeros give precisely $$(p_\ell,t_\ell)=(0,0)\quad\text{or}\quad(1,1).$$ Thus every slack is nonnegative at the chosen dimension. The assumed inequality forces every slack, and $\mathcal E$, to be zero.

A zero-slack face with $c=1$ has all pair dimensions equal to $1$. Any face across one of these pairs cannot be a lower-activity equality case from Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-lower-active" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-lower-active">26</a>, and cannot be a three-active equality case with $c=0$. It must have $c=1$ as well. Connectivity would give $h_e=1$ for every $e$, contradicting $\mathcal E=0$ at a pair with $a_e>1/3$. The remaining equality cases have $c_j=0$ and $h_e\in\{0,2\}$. As $S_j=0$, their gains equal their baselines, and $$\sum_jg_j=\sum_j\frac13\sum_{e\subset j}b(h_e)
          =\frac23\sum_e b(h_e).$$ The choices of $M_*$ and $C_*$ were uniform in the activity set and maximizing tuples, so the dimension threshold works for all of them simultaneously. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Two counts for the remaining pair spaces

It remains to improve the cost for the profiles in Equation <a href="#eq:tail-residual-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-residual-profile">[eq:tail-residual-profile]</a>. Their nonzero pair intersections are two-dimensional, and each pair space must lie in the Lagrangians at both endpoints. A dependence among the pair spaces forces at least one extra dimension in the sum of the local endpoint spans, producing a factor $q^{-D}$. For a direct family, the independent orthogonality constraints instead give the required cost after the endpoint containment factors cancel. The total ambient span need not be isotropic.

<a id="stmt-lem-tail-span"></a>
<div id="lem:tail-span" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (A strict span inequality). *Let $H_e$ be subspaces assigned to distinct pairs $e$ of a simple graph. Set $$k=\dim\sum_e H_e,\qquad k_i=\dim\sum_{e\ni i}H_e.$$ Then $\sum_i k_i\ge2k$. If the nonzero spaces do not form a direct sum, then $\sum_i k_i\ge2k+1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tail-span"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-span</summary>

*Proof.* For any ordering of the spaces, let $\Delta_e$ be the increment in the dimension of the global span when $H_e$ is added. At each endpoint $i$ of $e$, let $\Delta_{e,i}$ be its increment in the local span. The earlier local span is contained in the earlier global span, so $\Delta_{e,i}\ge\Delta_e$. Summing gives $\sum_i k_i\ge2\sum_e\Delta_e=2k$.

If the family is not direct, choose an inclusion-minimal subfamily $\mathcal T$ which is not direct. Order its members first and let $e$ be its last member. Put $$\delta_*=\dim\left(H_e\cap
          \sum_{f\in\mathcal T\setminus\{e\}}H_f\right)>0.$$ At least one endpoint $i$ of $e$ fails to belong to some other member of $\mathcal T$: otherwise every other pair in $\mathcal T$ would contain both endpoints of $e$ and would equal $e$. The members of $\mathcal T$ visible at this $i$, including $e$, are therefore a proper subfamily and form a direct sum by minimality. Consequently $$\Delta_{e,i}=\dim H_e=\Delta_e+\delta_*.$$ All other increment comparisons remain nonnegative, and hence $\sum_i k_i\ge2k+\delta_*\ge2k+1$. The argument uses the actual dimension $\delta_*$ and in particular includes a partial intersection of $H_e$ with the earlier span. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-tail-residual-counts"></a>
<div id="lem:tail-residual-counts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (Costs for the remaining profiles). *For a profile satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:tail-residual-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-residual-profile">[eq:tail-residual-profile]</a>, let $m$ be the number of pairs with $h_e=2$. The contribution of configurations whose pair spaces are not direct is $O_D(q^{-D+1641})$. The contribution of configurations whose pair spaces are direct is $$O_D\left(q^{-\frac13\sum_e b(h_e)}\right).$$ Here “contribution” includes the product of the center factors. The numerical remainder $1641$ is independent of $D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tail-residual-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-residual-counts</summary>

*Proof.* Write $H_e=L_i\cap L_k$ for $e=\{i,k\}$, and use $k,k_i$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-span" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-span">29</a>. We count possible $H_e$ and impose their containment in the independent $L_i$. Discarding exactness of the intersections can only enlarge the count. A nonisotropic local span cannot be contained in a Lagrangian and contributes zero. For every isotropic local span of dimension $k_i$, its containment probability is $O_D(q^{-Dk_i+\binom{k_i}{2}})$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:lagrangian-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lagrangian-counts">18</a>.

For the nondirect case, first choose an arbitrary $k$-space in the $2D$-space, in $O_D(q^{k(2D-k)})$ ways, and then choose the $m$ two-spaces inside it, in $O_D(q^{2m(k-2)})$ ways. Requiring their span to be the chosen space would only reduce this upper bound. On the stratum with fixed $k$ and $k_i$, multiplying the containment probabilities gives exponent $$-D\left(\sum_i k_i-2k\right)
 -k^2+2mk-4m+\sum_i\binom{k_i}{2}.$$ The point degrees in the pair graph, given by Proposition <a href="#prop:complex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:complex">4</a>, are $(4,6,4,6,5,4,5,5,5,6,5,6,5)$. Therefore $$\sum_i\binom{k_i}{2}
 \le\sum_i\binom{2\deg(i)}2=618.$$ Since $m\le33$ and $-k^2+2mk\le m^2$, the part of the exponent independent of $D$ is at most $$m^2-4m+618\le1575.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-span" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-span">29</a> makes the coefficient of $-D$ at least one. There are only boundedly many $k,k_i$, independently of $D$, because $k\le2m\le66$ and $k_i\le2\deg(i)$. Thus the probability of this part of the profile is $O_D(q^{-D+1575})$. Its total gain is at most $\tfrac23\sum_e b(h_e)=2m\le66$, proving the first bound. The ambient span in this argument was arbitrary; only the local spans were required to be isotropic.

For the direct case, the following count applies to arbitrary fixed dimensions $h_e$. Order the pairs and choose an ordered basis of each $H_e$. Each new basis vector must be orthogonal to the previous vectors of its own space and to all vectors of earlier spaces sharing an endpoint. In a direct family these previous vectors are linearly independent. Nondegeneracy of the symplectic form identifies ambient vectors with linear functionals, so their orthogonality equations are independent, even if the previous vectors are mutually orthogonal. If there are $a$ such equations, there are exactly $q^{2D-a}$ vectors satisfying them. Excluding choices which would destroy directness only decreases this count.

Let $e\sim f$ mean distinct pairs sharing an endpoint, and count each unordered pair $\{e,f\}$ once. Dividing the sequential basis count by the number of ordered bases in each space gives at most $$O_D\left(q^{\,2D\sum_eh_e-\sum_eh_e^2
 -\sum_e\binom{h_e}{2}-\sum_{e\sim f}h_eh_f}\right)$$ possible direct families satisfying all local orthogonality conditions. Here $k_i=\sum_{e\ni i}h_e$, so $$\sum_i k_i=2\sum_eh_e,
 \qquad
 \sum_i\binom{k_i}{2}
 =2\sum_e\binom{h_e}{2}+\sum_{e\sim f}h_eh_f.$$ Distinct pairs in a simple graph share at most one endpoint, which explains the coefficient of the last sum. Multiplying by the containment probabilities cancels both the $D$ terms and the cross terms. The remaining exponent is exactly $$-\sum_eh_e^2+\sum_e\binom{h_e}{2}=-\sum_e b(h_e).$$ The gain bound in Equation <a href="#eq:tail-residual-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-residual-profile">[eq:tail-residual-profile]</a> now leaves $-\tfrac13\sum_e b(h_e)$, as asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Completion of the rank-layer estimate

<a id="stmt-prop-singular-tail"></a>
<div id="prop:singular-tail" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:tail-vanishing"></a>
**Proposition 31** (Vanishing singular contribution). *For every sufficiently large even $D=2r$ and every activity set $B\subseteq\mathscr C$, $$\label{eq:tail-vanishing}
 \mathbb E_{L,Y}\left[
 \mathbf 1_{\{\exists e=\{i,k\}\in E:\,\dim(L_i\cap L_k)>0\}}
 \prod_{(i,j)\in B}J(L_i,Y_j)\right]=o(1)$$ as $q\to\infty$ through odd primes, with $D$ fixed. The dimension threshold can be chosen for all activity sets simultaneously.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-singular-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-singular-tail</summary>

*Proof.* Choose an even $D$ above the threshold in Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-residue" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-residue">28</a> and also with $D>1641$. These thresholds refer only to dimensions and the fixed complex. For any profile with some $h_e>0$, either $\mathcal C>\sum_jg_j$, or it satisfies Equation <a href="#eq:tail-residual-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-residual-profile">[eq:tail-residual-profile]</a>. In the first case, Equation <a href="#eq:tail-profile-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-profile-bound">[eq:tail-profile-bound]</a> gives a strictly negative $q$-exponent. In the second, the two bounds in Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-residual-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-residual-counts">30</a> are strictly negative because $D>1641$ and $\sum_e b(h_e)>0$.

At this fixed $D$, every selected gain is an integer and $6\mathcal C$ is an integer, because $3a_e$ is an integer. A strict first-cost saving is therefore at least $1/6$. A singular direct residual profile saves at least $1$, and the nondirect case also saves at least $1$ because even $D>1641$ implies $D\ge1642$. There are finitely many profiles and activity sets, and the gain exponents were selected from the fixed finite unions in Proposition <a href="#prop:planted-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:planted-gain">23</a>. Summing their vanishing contributions proves Equation <a href="#eq:tail-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-vanishing">[eq:tail-vanishing]</a>. The local maximizing tuples need not be realizable simultaneously: the residue argument uses only their necessary dimension inequalities and the shared profile dimensions. In particular, no simultaneous limit in $D$ and $q$ has been used. The bound $D>1641$ is needed here only for the nondirect residual case and is not asserted to be a threshold for the whole proof. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-singular-tail-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-singular-tail</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-layer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-layer">5</a>.* Choose an even $D$ large enough for both Proposition <a href="#prop:transverse-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transverse-limit">17</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:singular-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:singular-tail">31</a>. For the matrix variables let $$\mathcal T=\{\det(X_i-X_k)\ne0\text{ for every }\{i,k\}\in E\}.$$ On $\mathcal T$, Proposition <a href="#prop:transverse-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transverse-limit">17</a> gives the right-hand side of Equation <a href="#eq:rank-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-layer">[eq:rank-layer]</a>, up to $o(1)$, for every fixed activity set and every choice of incidence signs. The integrand is nonnegative. Lemma <a href="#lem:chart-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:chart-comparison">19</a> bounds its contribution on $\mathcal T^c$ by a constant depending on $D$ times the unsigned Lagrangian contribution in Equation <a href="#eq:tail-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-vanishing">[eq:tail-vanishing]</a>: graph intersections project isomorphically onto the kernels of matrix differences, and dropping the prescribed signs only enlarges the integrand after the comparable normalizations. This contribution is $o(1)$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:singular-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:singular-tail">31</a>. Adding the transverse and singular parts proves Equation <a href="#eq:rank-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-layer">[eq:rank-layer]</a>. There are only finitely many activity sets and sign assignments, so the convergence is uniform over the choices required in Section <a href="#sec:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:kernel">3</a>. This proves Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-layer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-layer">5</a>. The construction in Section <a href="#sec:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:kernel">3</a> therefore yields the strict symmetric-kernel inequality, and Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-host" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-host">10</a> gives a finite simple host $G$ with $t(H,G)<p(G)^{66}$, proving Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### Amplification and the forcing consequence

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is now proved. We derive the two consequences stated in the introduction directly from its finite host. The first uses tensor powers to amplify the homomorphism deficit and blow-ups to compare injective copies at a positive limiting density. The second keeps the edge density fixed while interpolating to an exact graphon equality, and then samples a nonquasirandom sequence of finite graphs.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### Amplification and injective copies

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-singular-tail-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-singular-tail</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:amplification">2</a>.* Take $G$ from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and put $N=|V(G)|$, $p_0=p(G)$, $t_0=t(H,G)$, and $\rho=t_0/p_0^{66}$. Simplicity and an edge give $0<p_0<1$, and mapping the two parts of $H$ to the endpoints of an edge gives $t_0>0$. Thus $0<\rho<1$.

Let $G^{\times r}$ be the categorical power, whose adjacency is coordinatewise. It is finite, simple and undirected, and homomorphisms into it factor coordinatewise. Hence $$p(G^{\times r})=p_0^r,\qquad
t(H,G^{\times r})=t_0^r,\qquad
\frac{t(H,G^{\times r})}{p(G^{\times r})^{66}}=\rho^r\longrightarrow0,$$ which proves (i). This is the standard tensor-power principle (Conlon, Fox, and Sudakov 2010, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[conlonfoxsudakov2010](#ref-conlonfoxsudakov2010)].

For (ii), use instead the balanced independent blow-ups $F_s=G[s]$: replace each vertex by an independent set of size $s$, and each edge by the complete bipartite graph between its two sets. Then $n_s=Ns$, $p(F_s)=p_0$, and $t(H,F_s)=t_0$, since each homomorphism into $G$ has exactly $s^{35}$ lifts. For $n_s\ge35$, set $q_s=\operatorname{Inj}(H,F_s)/(n_s)_{35}$. This is the homomorphism probability for a uniform vertex map conditioned on injectivity, so the collision union bound gives $$|q_s-t_0|\le1-\frac{(n_s)_{35}}{n_s^{35}}
\le\frac{\binom{35}{2}}{n_s}.$$ Meanwhile $d_s=p_0n_s/(n_s-1)\to p_0$. Therefore $q_s/d_s^{66}\to\rho<1$, proving (ii) with $p_*=p_0$ and $\delta_*=(1-\rho)/2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Failure of the forcing conjecture

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-singular-tail-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-singular-tail</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:forcing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:forcing">3</a>.* Let $G$ be the host from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, put $N=|V(G)|$, and set $p=p(G)$. Since $G$ has an edge and is simple, $$0<p\le\frac{N-1}{N}<1.$$ Let $W_0$ be its adjacency kernel on the uniform probability space $V(G)$. Independent vertex samples count all homomorphisms, so $$t(K_2,W_0)=p,\qquad t(H,W_0)=t(H,G)<p^{66}.$$ Moreover, $W_0$ is $\{0,1\}$-valued, and hence $\|W_0-p\|_2^2=p(1-p)>0$.

On the balanced sign space $\{-1,1\}$, choose $0<\delta\le\min(p,1-p)$ and define the rank-one perturbation $$W_1(a,b)=p+\delta ab.$$ It takes values in $[0,1]$ and has mean $p$. For $A\subseteq E(H)$, let $d_A(v)$ be the degree of $v$ in the edge set $A$. Expanding the product defining $t(H,W_1)$ and averaging the independent signs gives $$t(H,W_1)=
\sum_{\substack{A\subseteq E(H)\\ d_A(v)\text{ even for every }v}}
p^{66-|A|}\delta^{|A|}>p^{66}.$$ Indeed, the empty edge set contributes $p^{66}$, every surviving term is nonnegative, and the edge set of a cycle contributes a strictly positive term. Such a cycle exists because a forest on $35$ vertices has at most $34$ edges, whereas $H$ has $66$.

Lift the two kernels to the independent coordinates of $\Omega=V(G)\times\{-1,1\}$, with product probability, and set $$W_s\bigl((x,a),(y,b)\bigr)
=(1-s)W_0(x,y)+sW_1(a,b),\qquad 0\le s\le1.$$ Each $W_s$ is symmetric, takes values in $[0,1]$, and has mean $p$. The endpoint lifts preserve their $H$-densities. The two centered summands depend on independent coordinate pairs and have mean zero, so they are orthogonal in $L^2(\Omega^2)$. Thus $$\|W_s-p\|_2^2=(1-s)^2p(1-p)+s^2\delta^2>0
\qquad(0\le s\le1).$$ The function $s\mapsto t(H,W_s)$ is a polynomial. Its endpoint values lie strictly below and above $p^{66}$, respectively, so some $s_*\in(0,1)$ satisfies $t(H,W_{s_*})=p^{66}$. The norm identity shows that $W=W_{s_*}$ is nonconstant.

For completeness, this gives failure of asymptotic quasirandomness, not just a graphon equality. On the finite atom space of $W$, put $$r(x)=\mathbb E_z W(x,z),\qquad c(x,y)=\mathbb E_z W(x,z)W(y,z).$$ Two applications of Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\begin{aligned}
t(C_4,W)&=\mathbb E_{x,y}c(x,y)^2\\
&\ge\bigl(\mathbb E_{x,y}c(x,y)\bigr)^2
=\bigl(\mathbb E_z r(z)^2\bigr)^2\ge p^4.
\end{aligned}$$ Equality would force $r(x)=p$ for every $x$ and $c(x,y)=p^2$ for every pair $(x,y)$: every atom pair has positive mass, including the diagonal pairs. Then, for every $x$, $$\mathbb E_z\bigl(W(x,z)-p\bigr)^2=c(x,x)-2pr(x)+p^2=0,$$ contrary to nonconstancy. Hence $t(C_4,W)>p^4$.

Use the independent sampling construction for this finite kernel, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-host" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-host">10</a> and (Lovász and Szegedy 2006, sec. 2.6, Lemma 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[lovaszszegedy2006](#ref-lovaszszegedy2006)]. For each fixed finite graph $F$, the collision count gives $\mathbb Et(F,G_n)=t(F,W)+O_F(n^{-1})$. The covariance of the indicators for two vertex maps vanishes when their images are disjoint, and the proportion of pairs with overlapping images is $O_F(n^{-1})$. Therefore $\operatorname{Var}(t(F,G_n))=O_F(n^{-1})$. With tolerance $n^{-1/4}$, Chebyshev’s inequality and a union bound for $F=K_2,H,C_4$ give total failure probability $O(n^{-1/2})<1$ for all sufficiently large $n$. Choose one successful realization for each such $n$. The expectation estimate then gives a deterministic sequence of finite simple graphs with $$p(G_n)\to p,\qquad t(H,G_n)\to p^{66},\qquad
t(C_4,G_n)\to t(C_4,W)>p^4.$$ This sequence is not quasirandom of density $p$, proving the claim. ◻

</details>

The graphon equality is exact, whereas the finite sequence matches the edge and $H$-densities only in the limit. The argument supplies one density $p\in(0,1)$ and does not assert failure of $p$-forcing at every density.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Logical dependencies of the counterexample. The fixed triple system supplies the combinatorial properties used in both branches. The rank-layer limit realizes the averaged coefficient made negative by the finite activation construction. Section 3 constructs the kernel and transfers its strict deficit to a finite simple host assuming Lemma 5; Sections 4–7 then prove that input.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-overview",
      "pdf_page": 3,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-overview",
          "exact_quote": "Logical dependencies of the counterexample. The fixed triple\nsystem supplies the combinatorial properties used in both branches.\nThe rank-layer limit realizes the averaged coefficient made negative\nby the finite activation construction. Sec",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  font=\\small,\n  proofbox/.style={draw=linkblue!70,rounded corners=2pt,\n    fill=linkblue!4,align=center,inner sep=5pt,text width=3.4cm},\n  proofstep/.style={-{Latex[length=2mm]},thick,draw=linkblue!80}]\n  \\node[proofbox,text width=6.0cm] (complex) at (0,0)\n    {Fixed triple system\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:complex}};\n  \\node[proofbox,text width=3.8cm] (activation) at (-3.7,-1.5)\n    {Activation supports\\\\and type scores\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:kernel}};\n  \\node[proofbox] (transverse) at (0.9,-1.5)\n    {Transverse contribution\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:transverse}};\n  \\node[proofbox] (tail) at (4.9,-1.5)\n    {Weighted singular\\\\bound\\\\Sections~\\ref{sec:orbits}--\\ref{sec:tail}};\n  \\node[proofbox,text width=5.8cm] (rank) at (2.9,-2.9)\n    {Rank-layer limit\\\\Lemma~\\ref{lem:rank-layer}};\n  \\node[proofbox,text width=6.5cm] (negative) at (0,-4.3)\n    {Negative averaged coefficient\\\\in one limiting oriented density};\n  \\node[proofbox,text width=6.5cm] (kernel) at (0,-5.6)\n    {Dilution and crossed product\\\\symmetric kernel with a strict deficit};\n  \\node[proofbox,text width=6.5cm] (host) at (0,-6.9)\n    {Sampling a finite simple host $G$\\\\Lemma~\\ref{lem:finite-host}};\n  \\coordinate (estimatefork) at (2.9,-0.7);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (complex.south west) -- (activation.north);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (complex.south east) -- (estimatefork) -- (transverse.north);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (estimatefork) -- (tail.north);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (transverse.south) -- (rank.north west);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (tail.south) -- (rank.north east);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (activation.south) -- (negative.north west);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (rank.south) -- (negative.north east);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (negative) -- (kernel);\n  \\draw[proofstep] (kernel) -- (host);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart of logical dependencies with rounded rectangular boxes and connecting arrows. Top box: \"Fixed triple system / Section 2\". Directed arrows lead from it down to three boxes in a row: \"Activation supports and type scores / Section 3\" (left), \"Transverse contribution / Section 4\" (center), and \"Weighted singular bound / Sections 5–7\" (right). Arrows from \"Transverse contribution\" and \"Weighted singular bound\" point to a box below them: \"Rank-layer limit / Lemma 3.1\". An arrow from \"Activation supports and type scores\" and an arrow from \"Rank-layer limit\" both point down to: \"Negative averaged coefficient in one limiting oriented density\". From there, a downward arrow leads to: \"Dilution and crossed product / symmetric kernel with a strict deficit\", which points with a downward arrow to the bottom box: \"Sampling a finite simple host G / Lemma 3.6\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The seven coordinates available at the corner $κ = (v,j)$, where $j = {v, w, z}$, $e = {v, w}$ and $f = {v, z}$. The opposite faces on $e$ and $f$ are $l$ and $m$. The two activation branches keep the same six outer coordinates; one also keeps the common corner marked by the dot.",
      "figure_id": "fig:kernel-corner-activation",
      "pdf_page": 12,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:kernel-corner-activation",
          "exact_quote": "The seven coordinates available at the corner\n$\\kappa=(v,j)$, where $j=\\{v,w,z\\}$, $e=\\{v,w\\}$ and\n$f=\\{v,z\\}$. The opposite faces on $e$ and $f$ are $l$ and $m$.\nThe two activation branches keep the same six outer coordinates;\none also kee",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\\small]\n  \\fill[blue!7] (-1.25,0) ellipse (2.05 and 1.45);\n  \\fill[orange!9] (1.25,0) ellipse (2.05 and 1.45);\n  \\draw[blue!60!black,thick] (-1.25,0) ellipse (2.05 and 1.45);\n  \\draw[orange!70!black,thick] (1.25,0) ellipse (2.05 and 1.45);\n  \\node[blue!60!black] at (-2.4,1.35) {$U_e$};\n  \\node[orange!70!black] at (2.4,1.35) {$U_f$};\n  \\node at (-1.7,.75) {$(w,j)$};\n  \\node at (-2.0,-.1) {$(v,l)$};\n  \\node at (-1.45,-.85) {$(w,l)$};\n  \\node at (1.7,.75) {$(z,j)$};\n  \\node at (2.0,-.1) {$(v,m)$};\n  \\node at (1.45,-.85) {$(z,m)$};\n  \\fill[linkblue] (0,.55) circle (1.7pt);\n  \\node[align=center] at (0,-.05) {$\\kappa$\\\\$=(v,j)$};\n  \\node at (0,-1.9) {$M_\\kappa=U_e\\cup U_f,\\qquad\n                    \\Gamma_\\kappa=M_\\kappa\\setminus\\{\\kappa\\}$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Venn diagram with two overlapping shaded ellipses. Left ellipse labeled $U_e$ at top left; right ellipse labeled $U_f$ at top right. The overlapping central region contains a dark dot labeled \"$\\kappa = (v, j)$\". The left ellipse contains three non-central points: $(w, j)$ near top, $(v, l)$ at center-left, and $(w, l)$ near bottom. The right ellipse contains three non-central points: $(z, j)$ near top, $(v, m)$ at center-right, and $(z, m)$ near bottom. Below the diagram is the text formula: \"$M_\\kappa = U_e \\cup U_f, \\quad \\Gamma_\\kappa = M_\\kappa \\setminus \\{\\kappa\\}$\"."
    }
